).

2.1.2
4d effective action: NSNS and RR fluxes

On top of the above orientifold background one may add RR and NSNS fluxes. Using the democractic formulation introduced previously, the Bianchi identities for the field strengths read

\ell_{s}^{2}\,d(e^{-B}\wedge{\bf G})=-\sum_{\alpha}\lambda\left[\delta(\Pi_{\alpha})\right]\wedge e^{\frac{\ell_{s}^{2}}{2\pi}F_{\alpha}}\,,\qquad dH=0\,,

(2.24)

with
\ell_{s}=2\pi\sqrt{{\alpha}^{\prime}}
the string length. Here
\Pi_{\alpha}
hosts a D-brane source with a quantised worldvolume flux
F_{\alpha}
, and
\delta(\Pi_{\alpha})
is the bump
\delta
-function form with support on
\Pi_{\alpha}
and indices transverse to it, such that
\ell_{s}^{p-9}{\delta}(\Pi_{\alpha})
lies in the Poincaré dual class to
[\Pi_{\alpha}]
. O6-planes contribute as D6-branes but with minus four times their charge and
F_{\alpha}\equiv 0
. Finally,
\lambda
is the operator that reverses the order of the indices of a
p
-form. As introduced in (
2.5
),the solution to (
2.24
) can then be decomposed as

{\bf G}=e^{B}\wedge(d{\bf A}+\overline{\bf G})\,,\hskip 22.99988ptH=dB+\overline{H}\,,

(2.25)

where
{\bf A}={\bf C}\wedge e^{-B}
and
\overline{\bf G}
is a sum of closed
p
-forms to be thought as the background values for the internal RR fluxes. One may now impose Page charge quantisation
[
46
]
,

\frac{1}{\ell_{s}^{2p-1}}\int_{\pi_{2p}}dA_{2p-1}+\overline{G}_{2p}\in\mathbb{Z},\qquad\frac{1}{\ell_{s}^{2}}\int_{\pi_{3}}dB+\overline{H}\in\mathbb{Z},

(2.26)

where
\pi_{2p}
with
p=1,2,3
and
\pi_{3}
are internal cycles of
\mathcal{M}_{6}
. In the absence of localised sources such as D-branes, the gauge potentials
{\bf A}
are well-defined everywhere and the cohomology class of
\overline{G}_{2p}
,
\overline{H}
along
\mathcal{M}_{6}
capture the internal flux quanta. For orientifold compactifications the internal
p
-cycles have to comply with the orientifold projection, such that
the background flux can be characterised by virtue of flux quanta
(m,m^{a},e_{a},e_{0})
. These are defined as

m\,=\,\ell_{s}G_{0}\,,\quad m^{a}\,=\,\frac{1}{\ell_{s}^{5}}\int_{X_{6}}\bar{G}_{2}\wedge\tilde{\omega}^{a}\,,\quad e_{a}\,=\,-\frac{1}{\ell_{s}^{5}}\int_{X_{6}}\bar{G}_{4}\wedge\omega_{a}\,,\quad e_{0}\,=\,-\frac{1}{\ell_{s}^{5}}\int_{X_{6}}\bar{G}_{6}\,.

(2.27)

The internal RR-fluxes
{\overline{\bf G}}
are known to generate a perturbative superpotential for the Kähler moduli
[
47
,
48
]
:

\ell_{s}W_{K}=-\frac{1}{\ell_{s}^{5}}\int_{X_{6}}{\overline{\bf G}}\wedge e^{J_{c}}=e_{0}+e_{a}T^{a}+\frac{1}{2}{\cal K}_{abc}m^{a}T^{b}T^{c}+\frac{m}{6}{\cal K}_{abc}T^{a}T^{b}T^{c}\,,

(2.28)

where
{\cal K}_{abc}=-\ell_{s}^{-6}\int_{X_{6}}\omega_{a}\wedge\omega_{b}\wedge\omega_{c}
are the Calabi–Yau triple intersection numbers,
{\cal K}={\cal K}_{abc}t^{a}t^{b}t^{c}=6{\rm Vol}_{X_{6}}
and we have made used of the flux quanta defined in (
2.27
).
The NS 3-form flux
{\overline{H}}_{3}
on the other hand threads the
{\cal R}
-odd three-cycles
(B^{\mu})\in H_{3}^{-}({\cal M}_{6})
, which are the de Rham duals to the
{\cal R}
-odd three-forms
(\beta^{\mu})
introduced earlier. Similar to the RR-fluxes, the quantised Page charge for the NS-flux background can be expressed in terms of the integer flux quanta
(h_{\mu})
:

\frac{1}{\ell_{s}^{2}}\int_{B^{\mu}}{\overline{H}}=h_{\mu}\,,

(2.29)

and generate a linear superpotential for the complex structure moduli

\ell_{s}W_{Q}=-\frac{1}{\ell_{s}^{5}}\int_{{\cal M}_{6}}\Omega_{c}\wedge{\overline{H}}_{3}=h_{\mu}U^{\mu}\,.

(2.30)

The combination of RR and NS-fluxes suffices to generate a four-dimensional F-term scalar potential for the geometric moduli
(t^{a},u^{\mu})
and closed string axions
(b^{a},\xi^{\mu})
, given by

V_{F}=e^{K}\left(K^{\alpha\bar{\beta}}D_{\alpha}WD_{\bar{\beta}}\bar{W}-3|W|^{2}\right)\,,

(2.31)

where
D_{\alpha}W=\partial_{\alpha}W+W\partial_{\alpha}K
,
K=K_{K}+K_{Q}
-computed in (
2.18
)-(
2.20
)-,
W=W_{K}+W_{Q}
-see (
2.28
)-(
2.30
)- and the indices
\alpha,\beta=\{a,\mu\}
go over the complex and Kähler moduli. We will see in the next section
2.2
that this potential exhibits a remarkable factorisation into a geometric moduli piece, an axion piece and a flux piece
[
49
,
31
]
which is very useful to do a systematic search of vacua.

2.1.3
4d effective action: general fluxes

As we have already commented, type IIA compactifications with NSNS and RR fluxes represent a particularly interesting corner of the string landscape, as already the classical potential generated by
p
-form fluxes suffices to stabilise all moduli
[
50
,
33
]
. Even so, as pointed out in
[
51
]
one may consider a larger set of NS fluxes for this class of compactifications, related to each other by T-duality. Taking them into account results into a richer scalar potential, as analysed in
[
52
,
53
,
54
,
55
,
56
,
57
]
. In this last part of the derivation of the 4d effective action, we will consider the potential generated when we turn on geometric and non-geometric fluxes.

Again, we do not aim to give a detailed review of the topic -for that one can go to
[
56
,
58
]
- but just a minimalist introduction to motivate the scenario and fix the notation. The basic idea is that geometric and non-geometric fluxes appear when we consider T-dualities in flux compactifications. As it is known, T-duality relates a theory compactified on a circle of radius
R
with another theory compactified on a circle of radius
1/R
. An example of this symmetry is the duality between type IIA and IIB string theories, as pictorially presented in figure
1.2
. Subtleties appear when we play this same game in circles threaded by fluxes. Let us review the example studied in
[
51
]
. Take IIB compactified on a
T^{6}
with NSNS flux
H_{abc}
on some 3-cycle, with the indices
a,b,\dots\in\{1,2,\dots,6\}
denoting the internal directions. Performing a first T-duality, for instance in the direction
a
, the effect of the flux H is that in the IIA description the compactification is not on a torus but on a twisted torus. That is, in the dual picture, the flux is seen in the metric, which now has a component
\left(dx^{a}-f^{a}_{bc}x^{c}dx^{b}\right)^{2}
, where
f^{a}_{bc}\in\mathds{Z}
is the
geometric flux
. One can then perform a second T-duality, say in the direction
b
. The dual theory still admits a local description in terms of geometry (so we can define a metric locally) but globally the Riemannian description fails
4
4

4

Basically when one goes around the circle
x^{b}\rightarrow x^{b}+2\pi R^{b}
the metric and the B-field mix in a non-trivial way and the manifold is not globally well defined.
and the space is called
non-geometric
. In this exotic background, the presence of
H
is encoded through the flux
Q^{ab}_{c}
. One can perform a third T-duality, which yields a dual description that does not even admit a local description of the internal space in terms of geometry, and so is also non-geometric. We denote by
R^{abc}
the T-dual lux arising in this case in the 4d effective theory.

Figure 2.1:
(Non)-geometric fluxes and T-dualities.

It is important to point out that compactifications with non-geometric fluxes (
Q,R
) are not well understood. Though we will take them into account when constructing the 4d potential, when we look for vacua we will limit ourselves to just considering RR, NSNS and geometric fluxes.

The flux superpotential including geometric and non-geometric NS fluxes is described in terms of a twisted differential operator
[
53
]

{\cal D}=d-H\wedge+\ f\triangleleft+\ Q\triangleright+\ R\,\bullet\,,

(2.32)

where
H
is the NS three-form flux,
f
encodes the geometric fluxes,
Q
that of globally-non-geometric fluxes and
R
is the locally-non-geometric fluxes, see e.g.
[
56
]
for more details. The action of various fluxes appearing in
{\cal D}
is such that for an arbitrary
p
-form
A_{p}
, the pieces
H\wedge A_{p}
,
f\triangleleft A_{p}
,
Q\triangleright A_{p}
and
R\bullet A_{p}
denote a
(p+3)
,
(p+1)
,
(p-1)
and
(p-3)
-form respectively. Given these definitions, the NSNS flux-generated superpotential
W_{Q}
is now
[
53
,
52
]

W_{\rm Q}=-\frac{1}{\ell_{s}^{6}}\int_{X_{6}}\Omega_{c}\wedge{\cal D}\left(e^{J_{c}}\right)\,.

(2.33)

Expanding in the basis introduced in table
2.1
and using the action of the NS fluxes on such a basis as given in (
A.2
), one obtains the following expression for the superpotential

\displaystyle\ell_{s}W_{\rm Q}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle U^{\mu}\Bigl[h_{\mu}+f_{a\mu}T^{a}+\frac{1}{2}{\cal K}_{abc}\,T^{b}\,T^{c}\,Q^{a}{}_{\mu}+\frac{1}{6}\,{\cal K}_{abc}T^{a}T^{b}T^{c}\,R_{\mu}\Bigr]\,,

(2.34)

which, compared to (
2.30
), has some extra terms.
W_{K}
remains the same -recall equation (
2.28
)- and is not altered by the presence of (non)-geometric fluxes.

2.1.3.1
The F-term flux potential

Under the assumption that background fluxes do not affect the Kähler potential pieces (
2.18
) and (
2.20
),
5
5

5

The validity of this assumption should not be taken for granted and will depend on the particular class of vacua. The results in
[
59
,
60
,
2
]
suggest that it is valid in the presence of only
p
-form fluxes
F_{\rm RR}
,
H
. However,
[
61
]
gives an example of compactification with metric fluxes in which the naive KK scale is heavily corrected by fluxes, and so should be the Kähler potential.
one can easily (re)compute the F-term flux potential for closed string moduli via the standard supergravity expression (
2.31
). As in
[
49
,
31
]
, one can show that this F-term potential also displays a bilinear structure that will be exploited in chapter
4

2.1.3.2
The D-term flux potential

In the presence of a non-trivial even cohomology group
H_{+}^{1,1}
, geometric and non-geometric fluxes will generate a D-term contribution to the scalar potential. This can be computed as

V_{D}=\frac{1}{2}\left({\rm Re}f\right)^{-1\>\alpha\beta}\,D_{\alpha}\,D_{\beta}\,,

(2.35)

where
D_{\alpha}
is the
D
-term for the
U(1)
gauge group corresponding to a 1-form potential
A^{\alpha}
arising from
C_{3}
-recall decomposition (
2.21
)-

D_{\alpha}=i\partial_{{\cal A}}K\,\delta_{\alpha}\varphi^{\cal A}+\zeta_{\alpha}\,,

(2.36)

where
\delta_{\alpha}\varphi^{\cal A}
is the variation of the scalar field
\varphi^{\cal A}
under a gauge transformation, and
\zeta_{\alpha}
is the corresponding Fayet-Iliopoulos term. In order to find the explicit expression of the D-term potential we perform a gauge transformation on the gauge bosons in
C_{3}
6
6

6

We are focusing on
C_{3}
to maintain the discussion simple but, in the democratic formulation, similar operations have to be done for the 1-form
A_{\alpha}
arising from
C_{5}
.

A^{\alpha}\,\quad\longrightarrow\quad A^{\alpha}+d\lambda^{\alpha}\,.

(2.37)

The transformation of the RR
p
-form potential
C_{RR}\equiv C_{1}+C_{3}+\dots
can then be given in terms of the twisted differential
{\cal D}
given in (
2.32
)

\displaystyle C_{RR}

\displaystyle\longrightarrow

\displaystyle C_{RR}+{\cal D}\left(\lambda^{\alpha}\,\omega_{\alpha}+\lambda_{\alpha}\,\tilde{\omega}^{\alpha}\right)

(2.38)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left(\xi^{\mu}+\lambda^{\alpha}\,f_{\alpha}{}^{\mu}+\lambda_{\alpha}\,Q^{\alpha\mu}\right)\,\alpha_{\mu}+\dots\,,

where we have used the flux actions given in (
A.2
), with
f_{\alpha}{}^{\mu}
,
Q^{\alpha\mu}
,
f_{\alpha\mu}
,
Q^{\alpha}{}_{\mu}
integers. This transformation shows that the scalar fields
\xi^{\mu}
are not invariant under the gauge transformation, leading to the following shift in the
{\cal N}=1
coordinates
U^{\mu}
,

\delta U^{\mu}=\lambda^{\alpha}\,f_{\alpha}{}^{\mu}+\lambda_{\alpha}\,Q^{\alpha\mu}\,.

(2.39)

Note that due to the Bianchi identities (
A.2
) only the combinations of fields
U^{\mu}
invariant under (
2.39
) appear in the superpotential and, as a result, the Fayet-Iliopoulos terms vanish. Interpreting (
2.39
) as gaugings of the U(1) gauge fields and their magnetic duals one obtains the D-terms

D_{\alpha}=\frac{1}{2}\partial_{\mu}K\,\left(f_{\alpha}{}^{\mu}+\hat{\cal K}_{a\alpha\beta}b^{a}Q^{\beta\mu}\right)\,,\qquad D^{\alpha}=\frac{1}{2}\partial_{\mu}K\,Q^{\alpha\mu}\,.

(2.40)

Taking into account the kinetic couplings (
2.22
) we end up with the following D-term scalar potential

V_{D}=-\frac{1}{4}\partial_{\mu}K\partial_{\nu}K\biggl({\rm Im}\,\hat{\cal K}^{-1\>\alpha\beta}\left(f_{\alpha}{}^{\mu}+\hat{\cal K}_{a\alpha\gamma}b^{a}Q^{\gamma\mu}\right)\left(f_{\beta}{}^{\nu}+\hat{\cal K}_{c\beta\delta}b^{c}Q^{\delta\nu}\right)+{\rm Im}\,\hat{\cal K}_{\alpha\beta}Q^{\alpha\mu}\,Q^{\beta\nu}\biggr)\,,

(2.41)

where
\hat{\cal K}_{\alpha\beta}=\hat{\cal K}_{a\alpha\beta}\,T^{a}
. Alternatively, one may obtain the same potential by following the tensor multiplet analysis of
[
23
,
62
]
.
7
7

7

This result is different from the type IIA D-term potential of
[
57
]
, and recovers the expected discrete gauge symmetries related to
b
-field shifts. The same strategy can be applied to type IIB setups with non-geometric fluxes, recovering the full scalar obtained by DFT dimensional reduction in
[
63
]
.
The total potential has then two contributions

\displaystyle V=V_{F}+V_{D}\,,

(2.42)

though when we do a systematic search of facua in chapter
4
, we will consider only the case with
V_{D}=0
.

2.2
Flux potential, 4-forms and bilinear formalism

The main goal of this thesis is to shed light on the landscape of (massive) type IIA orientifold compactifications. To do that, a crucial ingredient is to understand the vacua structure of the flux potential arising in the 4d theory, see (
2.31
) and (
2.42
). In doing so, the bilinear formalism introduced in
[
49
,
64
,
31
,
65
,
66
]
turns out to be a very powerful tool, since doing a systematic search of extrema in this context is much simpler. In this section we will review and motivate briefly how the bilinear formulation was derived -for a detailed discussion the reader interested can go to the original references-. We will use this form of writing the potential in chapters
3
and
4
.

The importance of the 4-forms in the vacua structure of string theory can be traced back to Bousso and Polchinski (BP)
[
67
]
(building on previous ideas of Brown and Teitelboim
[
68
,
69
]
). They noticed that the existence of a large set of non-propagating 3-forms
C_{3}^{A}
in string theory could help to explain the smallness of the cosmological constant. The basic idea is that these 3-forms give rise to constant 4-forms (fluxes)
F_{4}^{A}=dC_{3}^{A}
that contribute to the vacuum energy as

\displaystyle V_{\text{effective}}=\sum_{A,B}Z_{AB}F^{A}F^{B}+\Lambda_{0}\,,

(2.43)

being
\Lambda_{0}
the bare cosmological constant (c.c). They showed that for a large enough number of 4-forms, one can construct in a natural way an exponentially small cosmological constant
V_{\text{effective}}
, without strong requirements in the bare c.c.
\Lambda_{0}
.

With this fact in mind, in
[
31
]
they studied the role of the four-dimensional 4-forms in massive type IIA flux compactifications. They showed that the different contributions to the scalar potential
V_{F}
can be written -just playing with the equations- as

\displaystyle 16S_{4d}\supset-Z_{AB}F_{4}^{A}\wedge\star_{4}F_{4}^{B}+2F_{4}^{A}\rho_{a}-Z^{AB}\rho_{a}\rho_{B}\,,

(2.44)

with the indices
A
running over the fluxes of the compactifications -the four-dimensional four forms
F_{4}^{A}
-,
\rho_{A}
depending on the axions and the topological data of the internal manifold and
Z_{AB}
depending only on the saxions. On shell
\star F_{4}^{A}=Z^{AB}\rho_{B}
and the scalar potential can be written as

\displaystyle V=\frac{1}{8\kappa_{4}^{2}}Z^{AB}\rho_{A}\rho_{B}\,,

(2.45)

which has a bilinear structure and is reminiscent of the (BP) structure. This was first obtained in
[
31
]
for massive type IIA compactifications with RR and NSNS fluxes, and then generalised in the presence of mobile D6 branes
[
65
]
and including
\alpha^{\prime}
corrections
[
66
]
. We will see in chapter
4
that even when we include (non)-geometric fluxes, the potential exhibits a bilinear factorisation.

It is important to stress that the bilinear expression for the potential is derived by just regrouping, defining, and rewriting some terms. There is no any hide assumption and the result is completely general. The point, as we will see, is that working with the
\rho
s one can perform quite easily a systematic search of vacua. We will give explicit expressions for them in the correspondent chapters.

2.3
Swampland conjectures

Finally, the last leg of this review of type IIA flux compactifications is the
swampland program
[
11
]
. As we have mentioned in the introduction, the swampland goes against the idea that any theory can be obtained from string theory. Contrary to this belief, quantum gravity is expected to constrain severely the kind of effective theories that are compatible with it. We say that effective theories that have a UV completion in quantum gravity are in the landscape, whereas those that do not have it are said to belong to the swampland. As of today, this field is actively in progress and we do not have many rigorous facts, but we work with
conjectures
, the so-called swampland conjectures. They can be derived in two ways. On the one hand, by thinking about which properties a theory of quantum gravity should satisfy. This is the case for example of the no global symmetries conjecture
[
70
,
71
]
, formulated using black hole arguments. On the other hand, analysing systematically string compactifications and extracting general patterns. An example of a conjecture derived in this way is the swampland distance conjecture
[
72
]
.

Under the swampland approach -for reviews see
[
73
,
74
,
22
,
75
]
- (massive) type IIA compactifications on CY orientifolds have received a renewed interest and are now being scrutinised. Among the different swampland conjectures, three of them involve these scenarios directly, the Non-Supersymmetric AdS Instability Conjecture
[
76
]
, the AdS distance conjecture
[
77
]
and the dS swampland conjecture
[
78
,
79
]
. Let us recall them briefly.

2.3.1
Non-Supersymmetric AdS Instability Conjecture

As it was formulated originally, it states
[
76
]

Any non-supersymmetric AdS geometry supported by flux is unstable in a consistent quantum theory of gravity with low energy description in terms of the Einstein gravity coupled to a finite number of matter fields.

The conjecture was not derived independently but obtained as a consequence of a refined version of the weak gravity conjecture (WGC). The vanilla version of the WGC
[
80
]
says that in any gravitational theory coupled to a
U(1)
with gauge coupling
g
, there must be a particle in the spectrum satisfying the inequality

\displaystyle m\leq\sqrt{\frac{d-2}{d-3}}gq\left(M_{p}^{d}\right)^{\frac{d-2}{2}}\,.

(2.46)

To see arguments in its favour, other versions of it and references, we refer the reader to the mentioned swampland reviews and
[
81
]
for a specific recent review of the WGC. The basic idea behind this conjecture combines the absence of global symmetries in quantum gravity, the evaporation of black holes and the violation of certain entropy bounds. These same arguments can be applied in theories with general
C_{p}
gauge fields, from which the existence of
p-1
dimensional objects follows, analogously to (
2.46
). In other words, the WGC states that in any theory of gravity coupled to a
p
-form Abelian gauge field, there must be a
p-1
membrane whose tension and charge satisfy

\displaystyle\sqrt{\frac{p\left(d-p-2\right)}{d-2}}T\leq QM_{p}^{\frac{d-2}{2}}\,.

(2.47)

In
[
76
]
a stronger claim was made and a refined version of the WGC was proposed, stating that the WGC (
2.47
) is saturated if and only if the theory is supersymmetric and the state in question is BPS. From this, it follows that any non-SUSY AdS supported by fluxes is at least metastable, as we will argue in a moment.

Consider a non-SUSY AdS
d
vacuum supported by fluxes
f=\int_{p-\text{cycle}}F_{p}
. Using Hodge duality, the fluxes
f
can be described in the
d
-dimensional theory in terms of top forms field strengths
F_{d}=dC_{d-1}
, where
d
is the dimensions of the spacetime. Now, the refined version of (
2.47
) applied to the
C_{d-1}
forms implies the existence of some
d-2
brane satisfying
T<QM_{p}^{2}
. But precisely, a co-dimension one brane whose charge is smaller than its tensions implies an instability in the AdS
[
82
]
, and so the vacuum can be at best metastable.

Type IIA orientifold compactifications provide a big arena where this conjecture can be tested. In chapter
7
we will examine some of the obtained non-SUSY vacua under the lens of this conjecture and will see how is non-trivially satisfied.

2.3.2
AdS distance conjecture

Originally formulated in
[
77
]
it states

Consider quantum gravity on d-dimensional AdS space with cosmological constant
\Lambda
. There existis an infinite tower of states with mass scale
m
which, as
\Lambda\rightarrow 0
, behaves (in Planck units) as

\displaystyle m\sim|\Lambda|^{\alpha}

(2.48)

where
\alpha
is a positive order-one number.

In string theory, the AdS distance conjecture is usually satisfied through Kaluza Klain modes, that is, the mass scale
m
appearing in the LHS of (
2.48
) can be identified with
M_{KK}
.

In the same original paper, a stronger version was proposed, claiming that

for SUSY AdS vacua
\alpha=\frac{1}{2}
.

Straightforwardly, from the strong version and the fact that
m\sim M_{KK}
and
\Lambda\sim M_{AdS}^{2}
it follows that
R_{KK}\sim R_{AdS}
and so SUSY AdS vacua with scale separation would be in the swampland. Though there are many examples in string theory supporting this form of the conjecture, IIA compactified on a CY orientifold does not satisfy it. We will see that they yield
\alpha=7/18
for both the SUSY and the non-SUSY AdS vacua, and so one can achieve
R_{\mathcal{M}_{6}}\ll R_{AdS}
parametrically.

As we have explained in the introduction of this chapter, the problem of AdS
4
orientifold vacua is that the complete solution to the 10d equations of motion is not known, because of the presence of intersecting orientifold planes. One usually works in the smearing approximation, where the localised sources are
smeared
along the internal dimensions -we will comment more on this approximation in the following chapters-. It is therefore an open question if the fully back-reacted solution still allows for separation of scales. In chapter
6
we will make some steps in this direction, showing that the smeared solution can be understood as the 0th order of an expansion whose 1st order we will construct.

In the meanwhile, there have been refinements of the strong AdS distance conjecture that make it compatible with scale separation
[
60
]
8
8

8

Though this version does not seem to be satisfied in 3d
[
83
]
.
, and the study of the would-be conformal duals of scale separated AdS vacua was initiated recently in
[
84
,
85
,
83
,
86
,
5
]
. There are other proposals that can be applied to these scenarios with scale separation, as the one of
[
87
]
, which states that SUSY AdS vacua whose mass of the lightest mode
m^{2}
is very large compared with the AdS mass scale
L_{AdS}^{-1}
,

\displaystyle m^{2}L^{2}_{AdS}\gg 1\,,

(2.49)

are in the swampland. In any case, as of today, it is still unclear if string theory admits solutions with separation of scales, which is crucial to constructing 4d theories describing our universe.

2.3.3
de Sitter conjecture

The refined version of the dS conjecture
9
9

9

Just after the original version of the conjecture
[
78
]
was released, several counterexamples were found -see for instance
[
88
]
- and so a refinement was proposed a few months later.
states
[
79
]

A A potential
V\left(\phi\right)
for scalar fields in a low energy effective theory of any consistent quantum gravity must satisfy either,

\displaystyle|\nabla V|

\displaystyle\geq\frac{c}{M_{p}}\cdot V\,

or

\displaystyle\text{min}\left(\nabla_{i}\nabla_{j}V\right)

\displaystyle\leq-\frac{c^{\prime}}{M_{p}^{2}}\cdot V\,,

(2.50)

for some universal constants
c,c^{\prime}\geq 0
of order 1, where the left-hand side of (
2.50
) is the minimum eigenvalue of the Hessian
\nabla_{i}\nabla_{j}V
in an orthonormal frame.

Though this is one of the most controversial conjectures, it is widely believed to be true at least in asymptotic regions of the moduli space (near-infinite distance singularities), where it is satisfied in all the cases studied. In other regions of the moduli space, it is not clear that the conjecture holds. Notice that it has strong implications in the description of our universe since it forbids stable dS vacua in string theory. For reviews of the current status of this problem, one can go to
[
89
,
90
]
. Related to this conjecture, we also need to mention the Transplanckian Censorship conjecture (TCC)
[
91
]
, which nevertheless we will not use in this work.

When looking for vacua, we will always have in mind the dS conjecture and will study what kind of set-ups could rise to violations of this conjecture. Anticipating the results, we will see first in equation (
3.6
) that the conjecture always holds in the presence of only NSNS and RR fluxes. Then, adding (non)-geometric fluxes, in section
4.3.2
, we will see that the conjecture could in principle be violated, though not with the ansatz we will consider.

Chapter 3
Search of vacua: RR and NSNS fluxes

A fundamental question in the context of string compactifications is the characterisation of the string Landscape, that is the collection of isolated, metastable 4d vacua that are obtained from string theory. In this regard compactifications with background fluxes
[
26
,
27
,
92
,
29
,
20
]
have proven to be a remarkably fruitful framework. To great extent, this is because they provide a simple mechanism for moduli stabilisation that at the same time generates a discretum of vacua, which allows developing our intuition on how the full string Landscape may look like.

Within the flux landscape, a very interesting corner is given by (massive) type IIA compactifications with RR and NSNS fluxes, in the sense that one may achieve full moduli stabilisation using only classical ingredients, as already mentioned. Early results on this subject display a non-trivial set of classical IIA flux vacua to AdS
4

[
93
,
94
,
95
,
34
,
96
,
50
,
97
,
33
,
98
,
99
,
100
,
101
,
102
,
103
,
104
,
105
,
106
,
107
,
108
]
. Some of these solutions are based on the results of
[
24
]
, which combines the classical Kähler potential of Calabi-Yau (CY) orientifolds and the superpotential induced by RR and NS background
p
-form fluxes to obtain an effective F-term potential, as reviewed in chapter
2
. In particular, ref.
[
50
]
obtains a discretum of
{\cal N}=1
AdS
4
vacua from such an effective 4d approach. The same strategy was implemented in
[
33
]
for the specific case in which the Calabi-Yau is a six-torus, finding different branches of supersymmetric and non-supersymmetric AdS
4
vacua.

In this chapter we extend the general analysis of
[
50
]
to find further vacua of the classical 4d potential of
[
24
]
, which are not necessarily supersymmetric. The motivation to analyse this particular setup is two-fold: on the one hand, it has been recently shown in
[
31
]
that the type IIA CY flux potential can be expressed as a bilinear on the flux quanta, in which the dependence of axions and saxions factorises -see section
2.2
-. As such, the extremisation conditions take a particularly simple form, already exploited in
[
65
,
66
]
in the search for new vacua. On the other hand, Calabi-Yau orientifolds with fluxes constitute an interesting arena to test the recent Swampland conjectures involving string compactifications to AdS
[
76
,
77
]
, and in principle they could provide counterexamples to them -recall
2.3
-. In order to properly address whether or not this is the case, it is important to determine the full set of vacua that corresponds to this construction.

Needless to say, solving for general vacua of a potential is more involved than restricting the search to supersymmetric ones. In the last case, even when the Kähler metrics for moduli fields are not fully specified, the vanishing conditions for the F-terms allow rewriting the vacua conditions algebraically, significantly simplifying the analysis. Interestingly, the factorised form of the potential found in
[
31
]
, which features a number of flux-axion polynomials invariant under discrete shift symmetries, allows implementing a similar strategy in the search of more general vacua. Indeed, we find that by imposing a simple off-shell Ansatz for the derivatives of the potential in terms of the flux-axion polynomials, the extrema conditions can also be expressed algebraically. By solving them we find several branches of extrema, one of which corresponds to supersymmetric AdS vacua, other to the non-supersymmetric Minkowski vacua discussed in
[
109
]
, and the rest are different branches of non-supersymmetric AdS solutions. Compared to previous results in the literature, on the one hand we find a one-to-one correspondence between our branches of solutions and those found in
[
33
]
for isotropic toroidal compactifications. On the other hand, we find that some of the extrema found in
[
108
]
are incompatible with our results. Our approach also permits to analyse the perturbative stability of these new AdS solutions, solving for the spectrum of flux-induced masses for the simplest branches of extrema. In those cases we find some branches where tachyons are absent, and some others where they are present but satisfy the Breitenlohner-Freedman bound. Finally, our strategy can be easily generalised to include moduli and fluxes in the open string sector, providing an even richer landscape of AdS flux vacua.

The rest of the chapter is organised as follows. In section
3.1
we rewrite the classical F-term potential associate to IIA Calabi-Yau orientifolds with fluxes in a bilinear formulation. In section
3.2
we implement our Ansatz to solve for the extrema conditions, finding several branches of solutions which are summarised in table
3.1
. In section
3.3
we analyse the perturbative stability of some of these branches and find that they can be considered perturbatively stable, see table
3.2
. Section
3.4
discusses the validity of these solutions from a 4d viewpoint. Section
3.5
generalises the setup to include D6-brane with moduli and the corresponding worldvolume fluxes. We draw our conclusions in section
3.6
, and relegate some technical details to the appendices. Appendix
A.1
contains some Kähler metric relations used in the main text, already introduced introduced in the previous chapter, while appendix
B
performs a detailed analysis of the Hessian for several branches of solutions.

3.1
Type IIA orientifolds with fluxes: bilinear formalism

In the following we will focus on (massive) type IIA flux vacua whose internal geometry can be approximated by a Calabi-Yau orientifold, as assumed in
[
24
]
to derive the F-term potential used in
[
50
]
.
1
1

1

Using such potential to search for vacua is justified a posteriori, by arguing that the flux-induced scale can be made parametrically smaller than the Kaluza-Klein scale, in the same region where corrections to the potential can be neglected, see
[
50
]
and section
3.4.1
. Therefore, even if in the presence of fluxes the compactification metric is not Calabi-Yau, it is expected that the fluxless Kähler potential is a good approximation to capture the 4d dynamics. See also
[
110
,
111
]
for some objections to this approach.
We will express the scalar potential directly in the factorised bilinear form of
[
31
]
. As pointed out in there, the bilinear form of the potential is independent on whether the background geometry is Calabi-Yau or not and, as it will be clear from the computations in the next section, so will be the strategy to extract the vacua from it.

Let us consider massive type IIA string theory compactified on an orientifold of
\mathbb{R}^{1,3}\times\mathcal{M}_{6}
with
\mathcal{M}_{6}
a compact Calabi-Yau three-fold. As discussed in section
2
, the 4d effective theory is described by a
\mathcal{N}=1
supergravity. The presence of RR and NS-fluxes suffices to generate a four-dimensional F-term scalar potential for the geometric moduli
(t^{a},u^{\mu})
and closed string axions
(b^{a},\xi^{\mu})
, whose precise shape exhibits a remarkable factorisation into a geometric moduli piece, an axion piece and a flux piece
[
49
,
31
]
-see section
2.2
-. Namely, we have a bilinear structure of the form

V=\frac{1}{\kappa_{4}^{2}}\vec{\rho}^{\ t}\,{\bf Z}\,\vec{\rho}\,,

(3.1)

where the matrix
{\bf Z}
only depends on the saxions
\{t,u\}
, while the vector
\vec{\rho}
only on the flux quanta and the axions
\{b,\xi\}
. More precisely, the dependence of the flux quanta is linear, and so one may write
\ell_{s}\vec{\rho}=R^{\prime}\cdot\vec{q}
, with
R^{\prime}
an axion-dependent rotation matrix and
\vec{q}=(e_{0},e_{a},m^{a},m,h_{\mu})^{t}
the vector of flux quanta. In general the entries of
\vec{\rho}
are axion polynomials with flux-quanta coefficients that are invariant under the discrete shift symmetries of the combined superpotential
W=W_{T}+W_{Q}
. In the case at hand they read

\begin{array}[]{lcl}\ell_{s}\rho_{0}&=&e_{0}+e_{a}b^{a}+\frac{1}{2}{\cal K}_{abc}m^{a}b^{b}b^{c}+\frac{m}{6}{\cal K}_{abc}b^{a}b^{b}b^{c}+h_{\mu}\xi^{\mu},\\
\ell_{s}\rho_{a}&=&e_{a}+{\cal K}_{abc}m^{b}b^{c}+\frac{m}{2}{\cal K}_{abc}b^{b}b^{c},\\
\ell_{s}\tilde{\rho}^{a}&=&m^{a}+mb^{a},\\
\ell_{s}\tilde{\rho}&=&m\,,\\
\ell_{s}\hat{\rho}_{\mu}&=&h_{\mu}\,.\end{array}

(3.2)

In this basis, the saxion-dependent matrix
{\bf Z}
reads

{\bf Z}=e^{K}\left(\begin{array}[]{ccccc}4&\\
&K^{ab}\\
&&\frac{4}{9}{\cal K}^{2}K_{ab}\\
&&&\frac{1}{9}{\cal K}^{2}&\frac{2}{3}{\cal K}u^{\mu}\\
&&&\frac{2}{3}{\cal K}u^{\nu}&K^{\mu\nu}\end{array}\right)\,,

(3.3)

where
K=K_{K}+K_{Q}
,
K_{ab}=\frac{1}{4}\partial_{t^{a}}\partial_{t^{b}}K_{K}
, and
K_{\mu\nu}=\frac{1}{4}\partial_{u^{\mu}}\partial_{u^{\nu}}K_{Q}
, and with upper indices denote their inverses.

3.2
Type IIA flux vacua

As already exploited in
[
65
,
66
]
, the bilinear structure of F-term potential (
3.1
) can be used to look for vacua in type IIA flux compactifications. In this section we will generalise this approach and implement a quite general strategy for the search of extrema of
V
, that will lead to different branches of solutions for the case of CY orientifold flux backgrounds. These branches will mostly describe new non-supersymmetric AdS solutions, but they will also contain the supersymmetric AdS solutions of
[
50
]
and the non-supersymmetric Minkowski solutions of
[
109
]
. As we will see, these vacua correspond to the branches of the toroidal type IIA flux vacua found in
[
33
]
, but now generalised to the much broader context of Calabi-Yau geometries. In the next section we will analyse the spectrum of some of these extrema and see that they are, in fact, classically stable AdS vacua.

3.2.1
Extrema conditions

Let us start by writing explicitly the different extrema conditions, grouped into the first order derivatives of the F-term potential (
3.1
) with respect to the axions
\{\xi^{\mu},b^{a}\}
and saxions
\{u^{\mu},t^{a}\}
of the compactification. Using the explicit expressions for
Z
and
\vec{\rho}
we find:

Axionic directions

\left.\frac{\partial V}{\partial\xi^{\mu}}\right|_{\rm vac}=\left.8e^{K}\rho_{0}\hat{\rho}_{\mu}\right|_{\rm vac}=0

(3.4a)

\left.\frac{\partial V}{\partial b^{a}}\right|_{\rm vac}=e^{K}\left[8\rho_{0}\rho_{a}+\frac{8}{9}\mathcal{K}^{2}\tilde{\rho}^{c}K_{ca}\tilde{\rho}+2\rho_{c}K^{cd}\mathcal{K}_{dla}\tilde{\rho}^{l}\right]_{\rm vac}=0

(3.4b)

Saxionic directions

\left.\frac{\partial V}{\partial u^{\mu}}\right|_{\rm vac}=e^{K}\left[e^{-K}V\partial_{\mu}K+\frac{4}{3}{\mathcal{K}}{\tilde{\rho}}{\hat{\rho}}_{\mu}+\partial_{\mu}K^{\kappa\sigma}{\hat{\rho}}_{\kappa}{\hat{\rho}}_{\sigma}\right]_{\rm vac}=0

(3.5a)

\left.\frac{\partial V}{\partial t^{a}}\right|_{\rm vac}=e^{K}\left[e^{-K}V\partial_{a}K+\partial_{a}\left(\frac{4}{9}\mathcal{K}^{2}\tilde{\rho}^{b}\tilde{\rho}^{c}K_{bc}\right)+\partial_{a}K^{cd}\rho_{c}\rho_{d}+\mathcal{K}_{a}\tilde{\rho}\left(\frac{2}{3}\mathcal{K}\tilde{\rho}+4u^{\mu}\hat{\rho}_{\mu}\right)\right]_{\rm vac}=0

(3.5b)

Interestingly, manipulating these condition one may rederive the inequality found in
[
112
]
that in turn prevents the existence of de Sitter vacua. Indeed, using the properties listed in appendix
A.1
it is straightforward to see that, off-shell:

\displaystyle u^{\mu}\partial_{u^{\mu}}V+\frac{1}{3}t^{a}\partial_{t^{a}}V=-3V-\frac{8e^{K}}{27}\mathcal{K}^{2}\tilde{\rho}^{a}\tilde{\rho}^{b}K_{ab}-8e^{K}\rho_{0}^{2}-\frac{4e^{K}}{3}K^{ab}\rho_{a}\rho_{b}\,.

(3.6)

At each extremum, where
{\partial}V=0
, this equation shows that
V|_{\text{extremum}}
must be negative or vanishing. In particular at a vacuum
V|_{\text{vac}}\leq 0
, forbidding any dS vacuum at the classical level. It would be interesting to see if the above kind of relation is preserved or violated by the different corrections to the classical approximation, along the lines of
[
113
]
.

3.2.2
The Ansatz

Rather than solving the extrema conditions (
3.4
) and (
3.5
) by brute force, in the following we will use the algebraic properties of the axion polynomials
\rho_{A}
to set up an Ansatz to look for vacua. To describe such Ansatz, we will first convert the vector
\vec{\rho}
into a different vector
\vec{\gamma}
, of the form

\vec{\rho}\ \rightarrow\ \vec{{\gamma}}\,=\,\left(\begin{array}[]{c}{\gamma}_{0}\\
{\gamma}_{a}\\
\tilde{{\gamma}}^{a}\\
\hat{\gamma}_{\mu}\\
\tilde{\rho}\end{array}\right)\,=\,\left(\begin{array}[]{c}\rho_{0}-\tilde{\rho}\epsilon_{0}\\
\rho_{a}-\tilde{\rho}{\epsilon}_{a}\\
\tilde{\rho}^{a}-\tilde{\rho}\tilde{{\epsilon}}^{a}\\
\hat{\rho}_{\mu}-\tilde{\rho}\hat{\epsilon}_{\mu}\\
\tilde{\rho}\end{array}\right)\,,

(3.7)

which can be seen as a (field-dependent) change of basis. The moduli-dependent functions
{\epsilon}
are such that
\vec{\gamma}
has only one non-vanishing component at the vacuum. Namely we define them such that
\vec{\gamma}|_{\rm vac}=(0\quad 0\quad 0\quad 0\quad\tilde{\rho})^{t}
. Of course, this does not really constrain what the
\epsilon
’s may be, because there is an infinite number of functions with the same value at a single point. However, we will impose an Ansatz that will significantly constrain this freedom. Indeed, in the following we will look at vacua such that, off-shell,

\boxed{\partial_{\alpha}V\,=\,\chi_{\alpha}^{A}\gamma_{A}}\,

(3.8)

where
\gamma_{A}=\{\gamma_{0},\gamma_{a},\tilde{\gamma}^{a},\hat{\gamma}_{\mu}\}
runs over all the components of
\vec{\gamma}
except
\tilde{\rho}
, and
\chi_{\alpha}^{A}
are some regular functions of the moduli, with the latter indexed by
\alpha
. Notice that this essentially implies that the
\epsilon_{A}
are also regular functions of the moduli.

In order to implement this Ansatz, it proves useful to rewrite the potential and the extrema conditions in terms of the
\vec{\gamma}
basis. We have that

V=\frac{1}{\kappa_{4}^{2}}\vec{\gamma}^{\ t}\,\hat{\bf Z}\,\vec{\gamma},

(3.9)

where, unlike the
\rho_{A}
, the elements of
\vec{{\gamma}}
are regular functions that depend on both the axions and the saxions. The bilinear product now reads

\hat{\bf Z}=e^{K}\left(\begin{array}[]{c c c c c}4&&&&{\epsilon}_{0}\\
&K^{ab}&&&K^{ab}{\epsilon}_{a}\\
&&\frac{4}{9}{\cal K}^{2}K_{ab}&&\frac{4}{9}{\cal K}^{2}K_{ab}\tilde{{\epsilon}}^{a}\\
&&&K^{\mu\nu}&\frac{2}{3}{\cal K}u^{\nu}+K^{\mu\nu}\hat{\epsilon}_{\mu}\\
{\epsilon}_{0}&K^{ab}{\epsilon}_{b}&\frac{4}{9}{\cal K}^{2}K_{ab}\tilde{{\epsilon}}^{b}&\frac{2}{3}{\cal K}u^{\mu}+K^{\mu\nu}\hat{\epsilon}_{\nu}&\frac{1}{9}{\cal K}^{2}+{\alpha}\end{array}\right)\,,

(3.10)

where

{\alpha}={\epsilon}_{0}^{2}+K^{ab}{\epsilon}_{a}{\epsilon}_{b}+\frac{4}{9}{\cal K}^{2}K_{ab}\tilde{{\epsilon}}^{a}\tilde{{\epsilon}}^{b}+K^{\mu\nu}\hat{\epsilon}_{\mu}\hat{\epsilon}_{\nu}+\frac{4}{3}{\cal K}u^{\mu}\hat{\epsilon}_{\mu}\,.

(3.11)

The strategy will now be to extremise
V
in this basis, in order to obtain the different expressions for the
{\epsilon}
’s or, in other words, the functional dependence of
{\gamma}_{A}
. To each class of solutions will correspond a different class of vacua.

Notice that we can split the scalar potential as

V=V_{1}+V_{2}=\vec{{\gamma}}^{\,t}\,\hat{\bf Z}_{1}\,\vec{{\gamma}}+\vec{{\gamma}}^{\,t}\,\hat{\bf Z}_{2}\,\vec{{\gamma}}\,,

(3.12)

where

\hat{\bf Z}_{1}=e^{K}\left(\begin{array}[]{c c c c c}4&&&\\
&K^{ab}&&&\\
&&\frac{4}{9}{\cal K}^{2}K_{ab}&&\\
&&&K^{\mu\nu}&\\
&&&&0\end{array}\right)\,,

(3.13)

and

\hat{\bf Z}_{2}=e^{K}\left(\begin{array}[]{c c c c c}0&&&&{\epsilon}_{0}\\
&0&&&K^{ab}{\epsilon}_{a}\\
&&0&&\frac{4}{9}{\cal K}^{2}K_{ab}\tilde{{\epsilon}}^{a}\\
&&&0&\frac{2}{3}{\cal K}u^{\nu}+K^{\mu\nu}\hat{\epsilon}_{\mu}\\
{\epsilon}_{0}&K^{ab}{\epsilon}_{b}&\frac{4}{9}{\cal K}^{2}K_{ab}\tilde{{\epsilon}}^{b}&\frac{2}{3}{\cal K}u^{\mu}+K^{\mu\nu}\hat{\epsilon}_{\nu}&\frac{1}{9}{\cal K}^{2}+{\alpha}\end{array}\right)\,.

(3.14)

Note also that
V_{1}
is positive semidefinite, while
V_{2}
is not. Because
V_{1}
is quadratic on quantities that vanish at the vacuum, the extremisation conditions are equivalent to taking derivatives with respect to
V_{2}
only

{\partial}V|_{\rm vac}=0\quad\iff\quad{\partial}V_{2}|_{\rm vac}=0\,.

(3.15)

In this sense, our Ansatz (
3.8
) requires something stronger than (
3.15
). Namely that, off-shell,
{\partial}V_{2}
is a function which is at least linear in the
{\gamma}_{A}
. In the following we will classify the different classes of solutions that arise from this requirement.

3.2.3
Branches of vacua

Let us now turn to solve for the extrema conditions (
3.4
) and (
3.5
). As we will see, rewriting them in the form (
3.15
) makes it easier to classify the different branches of solutions. Later on we will discuss how such branches reproduce and generalise previous vacua found in the literature.

Axionic derivatives

Already from the initial expression (
3.1
), (
3.2
), (
3.3
), one can see that
V
depends quadratically on
\rho_{0}
, which is the only quantity that depends on the complex structure axions
\xi^{\mu}
. Moreover, as it depends linearly we have that

\partial_{\xi_{\mu}}V=8e^{K}\rho_{0}\frac{\partial\rho_{0}}{\partial\xi_{\mu}}=8e^{K}\rho_{0}\hat{\rho}_{\mu}\,,\qquad\partial_{\xi_{\mu}}V|_{\rm vac}=0\rightarrow\boxed{\rho_{0}|_{\rm vac}=0}\,.

(3.16)

Therefore, in our Ansatz (
3.7
) one may take
{\epsilon}_{0}\equiv 0
, as we will do in the following.

Let us now look at the derivative with respect to the B-field axions:

{\partial}_{b^{a}}V_{2}=\tilde{\rho}^{2}e^{K}\left[\frac{8}{9}{\cal K}^{2}K_{ab}\tilde{{\epsilon}}^{b}+2K^{bd}{\epsilon}_{d}{\cal K}_{abc}\tilde{\epsilon}^{c}\right]+\dots

(3.17)

where we have used that
{\partial}_{b^{a}}\rho_{b}={\cal K}_{abc}\tilde{\rho}^{c}
and
{\partial}_{b^{a}}\tilde{\rho}^{b}=\tilde{\rho}{\delta}_{a}^{b}
, and the dots stand for terms linear in the
\gamma_{A}
. The Ansatz (
3.8
) has then two possible solutions:

•

Branch A1:

\displaystyle\boxed{\tilde{\epsilon}^{b}=0}

\displaystyle\rightarrow

\displaystyle\tilde{\rho}^{b}\rvert_{\rm vac}

\displaystyle=0\,.

(3.18)

•

Branch A2:

Let us assume that
\tilde{\epsilon}^{b}\neq 0
and multiply (
3.17
) by
t^{a}
. Using the relations in appendix
A.1
, one sees that a necessary condition for the bracket in the rhs of (
3.17
) to vanish off-shell is

\displaystyle\boxed{\epsilon_{d}=-\frac{1}{4}\mathcal{K}_{d}}

\displaystyle\rightarrow

\displaystyle\rho_{a}\rvert_{\rm vac}=

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{4}\tilde{\rho}\mathcal{K}_{a}\,.

(3.19)

Replacing this result in (
3.17
) one obtains a 2nd condition:

\displaystyle\boxed{\tilde{\epsilon}^{a}=Bt^{a}}

\displaystyle\rightarrow

\displaystyle\tilde{\rho}^{a}\rvert_{\rm vac}

\displaystyle=Bt^{a}\,,

(3.20)

with
B\neq 0
some regular function of the moduli.

Saxionic derivatives

The saxionic derivatives conditions are, for the complex structure moduli:

\displaystyle{\partial}_{u^{\sigma}}V_{2}={\tilde{\rho}}^{2}e^{K}\left[{\partial}_{u^{\sigma}}K\left(\frac{{\cal K}^{2}}{9}+{\alpha}\right)+\left({\partial}_{u^{\sigma}}K^{\mu\nu}\right)\hat{\epsilon}_{\mu}\hat{\epsilon}_{\nu}+\frac{4}{3}{\cal K}\hat{\epsilon}_{\sigma}\right]+

\displaystyle+{\partial}_{u^{\sigma}}\left(\frac{4e^{K}}{3}\mathcal{K}\tilde{\rho}u^{\mu}+2e^{K}\tilde{\rho}K^{\mu\nu}\hat{\epsilon}_{\nu}\right)\hat{\gamma}_{\mu}+{\partial}_{u^{\sigma}}\left(\frac{8e^{K}}{9}\mathcal{K}^{2}\tilde{\rho}K_{ba}\tilde{\epsilon}^{b}\right)\tilde{\gamma}^{a}+{\partial}_{u^{\sigma}}\left(2e^{K}\tilde{\rho}K^{ab}\epsilon_{a}\right)\gamma_{b}\,.

(3.21)

Notice that if one contracts (
3.21
) with
u^{\sigma}
and uses that
u^{\sigma}{\partial}_{u^{\sigma}}K^{\mu\nu}=2K^{\mu\nu}
one obtains:

-\frac{e^{-K}}{4{\tilde{\rho}}^{2}}u^{\sigma}{\partial}_{u^{\sigma}}V_{2}=\frac{1}{2}K^{\mu\nu}\hat{\epsilon}_{\mu}\hat{\epsilon}_{\nu}+{\cal K}u^{\mu}\hat{\epsilon}_{\mu}+\left(\frac{1}{9}{\cal K}^{2}+K^{ab}{\epsilon}_{a}{\epsilon}_{b}+\frac{4}{9}{\cal K}^{2}K_{ab}\tilde{{\epsilon}}^{a}\tilde{{\epsilon}}^{b}\right)+\dots\,

(3.22)

where the dots stand for terms linear in
{\gamma}_{A}
.

Finally, the Kähler saxionic derivative reads:

\displaystyle{\partial}_{t^{a}}V_{2}=e^{K}{\tilde{\rho}}^{2}\left[{\partial}_{t^{a}}K\left(\frac{{\cal K}^{2}}{9}+{\alpha}\right)+\frac{1}{9}{\partial}_{t^{a}}{\cal K}^{2}+\left({\partial}_{t^{a}}K^{bc}\right){\epsilon}_{b}{\epsilon}_{c}+\frac{4}{9}{\partial}_{t^{a}}\left({\cal K}^{2}K_{ab}\right)\tilde{{\epsilon}}^{a}\tilde{{\epsilon}}^{b}+{4}{\cal K}_{a}u^{\mu}\hat{\epsilon}_{\mu}\right]+

\displaystyle{\partial}_{t^{a}}\left(\frac{4e^{K}}{3}\mathcal{K}\tilde{\rho}u^{\mu}+2e^{K}\tilde{\rho}K^{\mu\nu}\hat{\epsilon}_{\nu}\right)\hat{\gamma}_{\mu}+{\partial}_{t^{a}}\left(\frac{8e^{K}}{9}\mathcal{K}^{2}\tilde{\rho}K_{bc}\tilde{\epsilon}^{b}\right)\tilde{\gamma}^{c}+{\partial}_{t^{a}}\left(2e^{K}\tilde{\rho}K^{cb}\epsilon_{c}\right)\gamma_{b}\,.

(3.23)

Proceeding as before, one can contract (
3.23
) with
t^{a}
to obtain:

\frac{e^{-K}}{{\tilde{\rho}}^{2}}t^{a}{\partial}_{t^{a}}V_{2}=\frac{1}{3}{\cal K}^{2}-K^{bc}{\epsilon}_{b}{\epsilon}_{c}+\frac{4}{9}{\cal K}^{2}K_{ab}\tilde{{\epsilon}}^{a}\tilde{{\epsilon}}^{b}-3K^{\mu\nu}\hat{\epsilon}_{\mu}\hat{\epsilon}_{\nu}+\dots\,

(3.24)

where again the dots stand for terms linear in the
{\gamma}_{A}
and we have used that
t^{a}{\partial}_{t^{a}}K^{bc}=2K^{bc}
and
t^{a}{\partial}_{t^{a}}\left({\cal K}^{2}K_{ab}\right)=4{\cal K}^{2}K_{ab}
.
Notice that both the first line of (
3.21
) and of (
3.23
) depend on
\tilde{\rho}
but not on any other component of
\vec{\rho}
. As such, they cannot depend on the
{\gamma}_{A}
. Following our strategy, we will then demand them to vanish off-shell, ensuring our Ansatz (
3.8
) and therefore that
{\partial}_{u^{\sigma}}V|_{\rm vac}={\partial}_{t^{a}}V|_{\rm vac}=0
.

To proceed, let us consider the general Ansatz for
\hat{{\epsilon}}_{\mu}
:

\hat{{\epsilon}}_{\mu}=A{\cal K}{\partial}_{u^{\mu}}K+{\mathcal{K}}\hat{{\epsilon}}_{\mu}^{\rm p}\quad{\rm with}\quad u^{\mu}\hat{{\epsilon}}_{\mu}^{\rm p}=0,

(3.25)

where
A
is some function of the moduli, and the factor of
{\cal K}
has been introduced for later convenience. The term
\hat{{\epsilon}}_{\mu}^{\rm p}
is a ‘primitive’ component of
\hat{{\epsilon}}_{\mu}
. We will first consider the case where
\hat{{\epsilon}}_{\mu}^{\rm p}=0
, which we dub:

•

Branch S1:

[formula]

On the one hand the vanishing of (
3.22
) becomes

4A-8A^{2}=\frac{1}{9}+{\cal K}^{-2}K^{ab}{\epsilon}_{a}{\epsilon}_{b}+\frac{4}{9}K_{ab}\tilde{{\epsilon}}^{a}\tilde{{\epsilon}}^{b},

(3.26)

which we impose off-shell. On the other hand the vanishing of (
3.24
) reads

48A^{2}=\frac{1}{3}-{\cal K}^{-2}K^{bc}{\epsilon}_{b}{\epsilon}_{c}+\frac{4}{9}K_{ab}\tilde{{\epsilon}}^{a}\tilde{{\epsilon}}^{b},

(3.27)

to be understood also off-shell. Combining these two equations we find

\displaystyle K_{ab}\tilde{{\epsilon}}^{a}\tilde{{\epsilon}}^{b}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{2}+\frac{9}{2}A+45A^{2},

(3.28)

\displaystyle\frac{K^{ab}{\epsilon}_{a}{\epsilon}_{b}}{{\cal K}^{2}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{9}+2A-28A^{2}.

(3.29)

For the
Branch A1
one finds the following solutions:

\displaystyle A=\frac{1}{15}

\displaystyle\rightarrow

\displaystyle\boxed{{\epsilon}_{a}=\pm\frac{3}{10}{\cal K}_{a}}\,\boxed{\hat{{\epsilon}}_{\mu}=\frac{{\cal K}}{15}{\partial}_{\mu}K}\,

(3.30)

\displaystyle A=-\frac{1}{6}

\displaystyle\rightarrow

\displaystyle{\epsilon}_{a}=\pm i\sqrt{\frac{3}{4}}{\cal K}_{a}\,,

(3.31)

the second one being unphysical. For the
Branch A2
one finds

\displaystyle A=\frac{1}{12}

\displaystyle\rightarrow

\displaystyle B^{2}=\frac{1}{4}\rightarrow\boxed{\tilde{\epsilon}^{a}=\pm\frac{1}{2}t^{a}}\,\boxed{\hat{{\epsilon}}_{\mu}=\frac{{\cal K}}{12}{\partial}_{\mu}K}\,

(3.32)

\displaystyle A=-\frac{1}{84}

\displaystyle\rightarrow

\displaystyle B^{2}<0\,,

(3.33)

again the second solution being unphysical.

•

Branch S2:

[formula]

Finding solutions in this branch is in general more involved, as one needs some more specific information on the Kähler potential for the dilaton and complex structure moduli. Things however simplify if one considers a Kähler potential of the form

K_{Q}=-{\rm log}(2s)-2{\rm log}\left(\tilde{{\cal G}}(u^{i})\right)\,,

(3.34)

where
\tilde{{\cal G}}
is a homogeneous function of degree
3/2
on the geometric complex structure moduli. This kind of Kähler potential was used in
[
109
,
65
,
66
]
to construct
{\cal N}=0
Minkowski flux vacua. Since in this case the metric for the dilaton and other complex structure moduli decouple, it is natural to make the following Ansatz

\hat{{\epsilon}}_{0}=E_{0}{\cal K}{\partial}_{s}K=-E_{0}\frac{{\cal K}}{s}\,,\quad\quad\hat{{\epsilon}}_{i}=E{\cal K}{\partial}_{u^{i}}K=-2E{\cal K}\frac{{\partial}_{i}\tilde{{\cal G}}}{\tilde{{\cal G}}}\,,

(3.35)

with
E
,
E_{0}
functions of the moduli. Then we may easily derive two equations from (
3.21
), namely

\displaystyle{\partial}_{s}V_{2}=0

\displaystyle\rightarrow

\displaystyle 8E_{0}^{2}{\cal K}^{2}-\frac{4}{3}E_{0}{\cal K}^{2}=\left(\frac{1}{9}{\cal K}^{2}+{\alpha}\right)\,,

(3.36)

\displaystyle u^{i}{\partial}_{u^{i}}V_{2}=0

\displaystyle\rightarrow

\displaystyle 8E^{2}{\cal K}^{2}-\frac{4}{3}E{\cal K}^{2}=\left(\frac{1}{9}{\cal K}^{2}+{\alpha}\right)\,.

(3.37)

Notice that
E
,
E_{0}
are solutions to the same quadratic equation, so if
E\neq E_{0}
then necessarily

E+E_{0}=\frac{1}{6}\,.

(3.38)

Using this we can rewrite (
3.37
) as

-8E^{2}+\frac{8}{3}E={\cal K}^{-2}K^{ab}{\epsilon}_{a}{\epsilon}_{b}+\frac{4}{9}K_{ab}\tilde{{\epsilon}}^{a}\tilde{{\epsilon}}^{b}.

(3.39)

Moreover, from (
3.24
) and using (
3.38
) one obtains

48E^{2}-4E=-{\cal K}^{-2}K^{bc}{\epsilon}_{b}{\epsilon}_{c}+\frac{4}{9}K_{ab}\tilde{{\epsilon}}^{a}\tilde{{\epsilon}}^{b}.

(3.40)

To sum up, one finds the equations

\displaystyle K_{ab}\tilde{{\epsilon}}^{a}\tilde{{\epsilon}}^{b}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 9\left(5E^{2}-\frac{1}{6}E\right)\,,

(3.41)

\displaystyle\frac{K^{ab}{\epsilon}_{a}{\epsilon}_{b}}{{\cal K}^{2}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-28E^{2}+\frac{10}{3}E\,.

(3.42)

In the following we will analyse the possible solutions for the two axionic branches.

For the
Branch A1
one finds the following solutions:

\displaystyle E=0

\displaystyle\rightarrow

\displaystyle\boxed{{\epsilon}_{a}=0}\,,\ \boxed{\hat{{\epsilon}}_{0}=-\frac{{\cal K}}{6s}}\,,\ \boxed{\hat{{\epsilon}}_{i}=0}\,

(3.43)

\displaystyle E=\frac{1}{30}

\displaystyle\rightarrow

\displaystyle\boxed{{\epsilon}_{a}=\pm\frac{\sqrt{6}}{10}{\cal K}_{a}}\,\ \boxed{\hat{{\epsilon}}_{0}=-\frac{2{\cal K}}{15s}}\,\ \boxed{\hat{{\epsilon}}_{i}=\frac{{\cal K}}{30}{\partial}_{u^{i}}K}\,.

(3.44)

One can check that (
3.43
) corresponds to the Minkowski vacua analysed in
[
109
,
65
]
.

For the
Branch A2
one finds

\displaystyle E=\frac{1}{12}

\displaystyle\rightarrow

\displaystyle{\tilde{\epsilon}^{a}=\pm\frac{1}{2}t^{a}},\quad{\hat{{\epsilon}}_{0}=\frac{{\cal K}}{12}{\partial}_{u^{0}}K},\quad{\hat{{\epsilon}}_{i}=\frac{{\cal K}}{12}{\partial}_{u^{i}}K}\,

(3.45)

\displaystyle E=\frac{1}{28}

\displaystyle\rightarrow

\displaystyle\boxed{\tilde{\epsilon}^{a}=\pm\frac{1}{14}t^{a}}\,\ \boxed{\hat{{\epsilon}}_{0}=-\frac{11{\cal K}}{84s}}\,\ \boxed{\hat{{\epsilon}}_{i}=\frac{{\cal K}}{28}{\partial}_{u^{i}}K}\,.

(3.46)

Note that (
3.45
) is in fact a special case of the Branch S1. For all the other solutions one can express things in terms of the Ansatz (
3.25
) as

\hat{{\epsilon}}_{\mu}=\left(\frac{E}{2}+\frac{1}{24}\right){\cal K}{\partial}_{u^{\mu}}K+{\mathcal{K}}\hat{{\epsilon}}_{\mu}^{\rm p}\,,

(3.47)

with

\hat{{\epsilon}}_{0}^{\rm p}=\left(\frac{1}{8}-\frac{3E}{2}\right){\partial}_{s}K\,,\quad\quad\hat{{\epsilon}}_{i}^{\rm p}=\left(\frac{E}{2}-\frac{1}{24}\right){\partial}_{u^{i}}K\,.

(3.48)

So in total we find two (double) classes of AdS solutions in the Branch
S1
and two (double) classes of AdS solutions in the Branch
S2
, where in the latter we have assumed the factorised metric Ansatz (
3.34
).

Uniqueness of the solutions

Some comments are in order regarding the uniqueness of these solutions. An implicit assumption of the above discussion is that the Kähler metric
K_{K}^{ab}
is irreducible. If the metric display a block-diagonal structure, as for instance in toroidal orientifolds, then more solutions are recovered. Indeed, one can check that in that case the choice of sign for the
\epsilon_{a}
’s in (
3.30
) and (
3.44
) can be made independently on each block. Each choice corresponds in principle to a different solution, as it is related to different signs of the flux quanta. The election of the signs will not be reflected in the value of the
V_{\text{vacuum}}
- which is invariant - but it will affect the F-terms and the spectrum of light modes. Unless stated differently, in the following we will consider a generic irreducible Kähler metric, for which the choice of sign must be equal for all
{\epsilon}_{a}
’s.

3.2.4
Summary of the vacua and physical properties

Let us recap the previous results and compute some of the properties of these extrema:

General structure

All the solutions found for the vacuum equations satisfy:

\displaystyle\rho_{0}

\displaystyle=0\,,

\displaystyle\hat{\rho}_{\mu}

\displaystyle={\tilde{\rho}}{\cal K}\left(A{\partial}_{u^{\mu}}K+\hat{{\epsilon}}_{\mu}^{\rm p}\right)\,,

\displaystyle\tilde{\rho}^{a}

\displaystyle=B\tilde{\rho}t^{a}\,,

\displaystyle\rho_{a}

\displaystyle=C\tilde{\rho}{\cal K}_{a}\,,

(3.49)

with
A,B,C\in\mathds{R}
. The
Branch A1
has
B=0
, whereas the
Branch A2
has
B\neq 0
,
C=-1/4
. The
Branch S1
has
\hat{{\epsilon}}_{\mu}^{\rm p}=0
whereas the
Branch S2
has
\hat{{\epsilon}}_{\mu}^{\rm p}\neq 0
. It is convenient to point out that as long as
A\neq 0
,
C\neq 0
- ignoring the complex structure axions for the moment - there are as many equations as moduli so in principle all the moduli can be fixed. Regarding the complex structure axions, only the linear combination that appears in the superpotential (
2.30
) is fixed. As pointed out in
[
33
]
, this allows the remaining axions to participate in the Stückelberg mechanism present in the presence of space-time-filling D6-branes, while guaranteeing the gauge invariance of the flux superpotential.

Kähler moduli stabilisation

The structure (
3.49
) provides several relations between the Kähler moduli and the axion polynomials of the compactification. In particular, the last two equations involving
\rho_{a}
and
\tilde{\rho}^{a}
provide
2h^{1,1}_{-}
relations between the quantised zero-, two- and four-form fluxes and the complexified Kähler moduli. By using (
3.2
) one may derive an explicit relation between the geometric Kähler moduli and the quantised fluxes. Namely we have that

\hat{e}_{a}\equiv e_{a}-\frac{1}{2}\frac{{\cal K}_{abc}m^{a}m^{b}}{m}=\ell_{s}{\tilde{\rho}}{\cal K}_{a}\left(C-\frac{1}{2}B^{2}\right)\,,

(3.50)

where we have defined a shifted four-form flux
\hat{e}_{a}
analogous to the one in
[
50
]
, invariant under discrete shifts involving Kähler axions and fluxes. It follows from this relation that whenever
B^{2}=2C
one needs to impose
\hat{e}_{a}=0
in order to have a sensible solution for the extrema conditions, and that then the individual Kähler moduli are not stabilised. One can check that this is the case for the branch (
3.43
), corresponding to the non-supersymmetric Minkowski solutions analysed in
[
109
]
, see also
[
65
,
66
]
. As pointed out in there, for Minkowski vacua the constraint on the fluxes
\hat{e}_{a}=0
is lifted once that
\alpha^{\prime}
corrections for the Kähler sector are taken into account.

Vacuum energy

Using the expressions (
3.6
) and (
3.49
) it is straightforward to see that the vacuum energy has the following general expression:

\Lambda=V\rvert_{\rm vac}=-\left(\frac{2}{27}B^{2}+\frac{16}{27}C^{2}\right)\frac{e^{K}}{\kappa_{4}^{2}}{\cal K}^{2}{\tilde{\rho}}^{2}\,.

(3.51)

F-terms

Using (
3.49
) and the expression for the F-terms derived in
[
65
]
one can directly compute them for each of the above extrema

F_{T^{a}}={\tilde{\rho}}{\cal K}_{a}\left(-\frac{C}{2}-\frac{1}{4}+6A\right)+i{\cal K}_{a}{\tilde{\rho}}\frac{B}{4}\,,

(3.52)

F_{U^{\mu}}={\tilde{\rho}}{\cal K}{\partial}_{u^{\mu}}K\left(\frac{C}{2}-\frac{1}{12}-A+i\frac{B}{4}\right)+{\mathcal{K}}{\tilde{\rho}}\hat{\epsilon}^{p}_{\mu}\,.

(3.53)

Summary

Finally, we gather all the above results in table
3.1
:

).

As already mentioned, when the structure of the metric in the Kähler sector is block diagonal, this allows to choose the sign of
C
independently in each block and therefore the corresponding value of the F-term. In particular, in the
Branch A1-S1
one can then break SUSY independently in each of the block-diagonal sectors.

3.2.5
Relation to previous results

As a cross-check of formalism and the solutions discussed so far, let us compare them with some of the existing results in the literature. We will analyse three different papers, presenting their main results schematically. We refer the reader to the original papers for further details.

1.

Comparison with DGKT

[
50
]

This paper analyses the general conditions for
\mathcal{N}=1
Calabi-Yau orientifold vacua, which are then applied to the particular orbifold background
\otimes^{3}_{j=1}T_{j}^{2}/\mathds{Z}_{3}^{2}
. At the general level, one can easily map our conditions for the
A1-S1
SUSY branch with the equations of section 4 of
[
50
]
. For instance, the condition

\displaystyle{\tilde{\rho}}^{a}=0\rightarrow b^{a}=-\frac{m^{a}}{m}\,,

(3.54)

is equivalent
2
2

2

There are some signs differences which arise form the different conventions in the flux quanta definitions.
to (4.33) in
[
50
]
. This implies that

\displaystyle\rho_{a}

\displaystyle=\frac{3}{10}\tilde{\rho}{\cal K}_{a}\longrightarrow\hat{e}_{a}=\frac{3}{10}m{\mathcal{K}}_{abc}t^{b}t^{c},

(3.55)

which is equivalent to (4.36) in
[
50
]
. Regarding the dilaton/complex structure sector, on the one hand one can see that the equations (4.24) and (4.25) in
[
50
]
are equivalent to (3.37) of
[
65
]
and to the second condition in (
3.30
). On the other hand, one can check that the eq.(4.26) of
[
50
]
that fixes one linear combination of axions
\xi
is equivalent to
\rho_{0}=0
.

The same statements hold when applying the above to the specific background
\otimes^{3}_{j=1}T_{j}^{2}/\mathds{Z}_{3}^{2}
. Before the inclusion of fluxes, the moduli space of this compactification consists of the axio-dilaton and 12 complexified Kähler moduli: 3 of them inherited form the toroidal geometry, and 9 associated with the blow-ups of the orbifold singular points. Since there are no complex structure moduli, the only necessary inputs to solve our equations are the intersection numbers, given by:

\displaystyle{\mathcal{K}}_{ijk}

\displaystyle=\kappa\iff i\neq j\neq k\,,

\displaystyle{\mathcal{K}}_{AAA}

\displaystyle=\beta\,,

(3.56)

where
i,j...
label the toroidal Kähler moduli and
A,B...
the blow-up modes. Applying (
3.54
) and (
3.55
) to this model one finds

\displaystyle{\tilde{\rho}}_{i}=

\displaystyle\frac{3}{10}{\tilde{\rho}}{\mathcal{K}}_{i}\rightarrow t_{i}=\sqrt{\frac{5\hat{e}_{j}\hat{e}_{k}}{3m\kappa\hat{e}_{i}}}\,,

\displaystyle{\tilde{\rho}}_{A}

\displaystyle=\frac{3}{10}{\tilde{\rho}}{\mathcal{K}}_{A}\rightarrow t_{A}=\sqrt{\frac{10\hat{e}_{A}}{3\beta m}}\,,

(3.57)

with
\hat{e}_{i}=e_{i}-\kappa\frac{m_{j}m_{k}}{m}
,
\hat{e}_{A}=e_{A}-\beta\frac{e_{A}^{2}}{2m}
, which is equivalent to (5.5) and (5.8) in
[
50
]
. One can equally recover eqs.(5.10) and (5.12) from applying the conditions of the
A1-S1
SUSY branch. Therefore our results reproduce the analysis in
[
50
]
, as expected.

2.

Comparison with NT
[
108
]

This paper considers the same orbifold background as
[
50
]
, but searches for non-supersymmetric vacua as well. By approximating the potential to its leading terms in certain flux quotients, more solutions to the extremisation equations are found, which are labelled as
\{\text{Case 1), \ldots, Case 8)}\}
. Case 1) stands for the supersymmetric solutions already found in
[
50
]
. Case 2) is related to Case 1) by an overall sign flip in all the RR four-form fluxes, that is by an overall sign flip in the
\rho_{a}
or equivalently in the
{\epsilon}_{a}
. Therefore, Case 1) and 2) correspond to the two components of the branch
A1-S1
in table
3.1
. Finally, Cases 3), …, 8) are obtained by partial sign flips in the four-form fluxes corresponding to the toroidal and blow-up two-cycles, and some of these cases are identified as classically stable vacua while others are not.

However, one can check that once that the blow-up moduli are introduced the metric in the Kähler sector is irreducible. Therefore, from the viewpoint of our analysis, none of the cases 3), …, 8) would be actual extrema of the scalar potential. This can be seen for instance by means of the equation (
3.29
): performing partial sign flips in the
\epsilon_{a}
’s will change the LHS for an irreducible Kähler metric, while the RHS remains invariant. The fact that the analysis in
[
108
]
identifies these cases as extrema is presumably due to the approximations made in the potential, which effectively removes the kinetic mixing between the different Kähler modes.

3.

Comparison with CFI
[
33
]

In this case the CY orientifold is given by
\otimes^{3}_{j=1}T_{j}^{2}/\Omega_{p}\left(-1\right)^{F_{L}}\sigma
, so there are three complexified Kähler moduli, three complex structure moduli and the axio-dilaton. To find different branches of vacua the simplification
T_{1}=T_{2}=T_{3}=T
is imposed in the Kähler sector. The relevant data to apply our results are:

\displaystyle{\mathcal{K}}_{ijk}

\displaystyle=1\iff i\neq j\neq k\,,

\displaystyle K_{Q}

\displaystyle\sim-\log\left(u_{0}u_{1}u_{2}u_{3}\right)\,,

(3.58)

where we are using
i,j...
to label the Kähler moduli and
\mu,\nu...
to label the complex structure moduli (
U^{i}
) and the axio-dilaton (
U^{0})
.
The two branches
A1
and
A2
become:

\displaystyle\tilde{\rho}^{b}\rvert_{\rm vac}

\displaystyle=0\rightarrow b=-\frac{c_{2}}{{\tilde{\rho}}}\,,

\displaystyle\rho_{a}\rvert_{\rm vac}=

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{4}\tilde{\rho}\mathcal{K}_{a}\rightarrow b=\frac{-c_{2}\pm\sqrt{\Gamma-{\tilde{\rho}}^{2}t^{2}/2}}{{\tilde{\rho}}}\,,

(3.59)

respectively. Here, as in
[
33
]
, we have dropped the indices in the Kähler sector, renamed
m^{a}=c_{2}
,
e_{a}=c_{1}
and defined
\Gamma=c_{2}^{2}-mc_{1}
. Notice that these are precisely the two branches found in eq.(4.23) of
[
33
]
, up to some sign due to different conventions in defining flux quanta. Inside each branch, we have distinguished between the subbranches
S1
and
S2
that read:

\displaystyle\hat{{\epsilon}}_{\mu}^{\rm p}

\displaystyle=0\rightarrow\hat{\rho}_{k}u_{k}=\hat{\rho}_{0}u_{0}\,,

\displaystyle E+E_{0}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{6}\rightarrow\hat{\rho}_{k}u_{k}={\tilde{\rho}}t^{3}-\hat{\rho}_{0}u_{0}\,,

(3.60)

which are precisely the two sub-branches in eq.(4.24) of
[
33
]
. Once that we have matched the branches, is direct to see that, in the vacuum:

3.3
Perturbative stability of the solutions

Given the above families of extrema of the flux-induced potential, a natural question is which ones are actual vacua. In the following we would like to analyse this question at the classical level, by computing the spectrum of flux-induced masses on the former moduli fields. In particular, we will check whether the non-supersymmetric AdS extrema have any tachyonic direction with a mass below the BF found
[
114
]
. For simplicity, we will do this computation focusing only on the
A1-S1
and
A2-S1
branches, leaving the
S2
branch for further work. In chapter
7
we will go one step further and study the non-perturbative stability of branch
A1-S1
.

3.3.1
The Hessian

By construction, we have a potential whose first derivatives are of the form

\partial_{\alpha}V=\chi_{\alpha}^{A}\gamma_{A}\,,

(3.65)

with
\chi_{a}^{A}
some regular functions of the saxions and the
\rho
’s. Therefore we have that

{\partial}_{\alpha}{\partial}_{\beta}V|_{\rm vac}=\chi_{\alpha}^{A}{\partial}_{\beta}\gamma_{A}\,,

(3.66)

where we have imposed our extremisation conditions
\gamma_{A}=0
. In fact, since
V_{1}
is quadratic in the
\vec{\gamma}
,
\partial^{2}V_{1}|_{\rm vac}
must be quadratic in
\partial\vec{\gamma}
. Indeed, one easily sees that

\boxed{{\partial}_{\alpha}{\partial}_{\beta}V_{1}\rvert_{\rm vac}=2\left({\partial}_{\alpha}\vec{{\gamma}}^{\,t}\right)\hat{\bf Z}_{1}\left({\partial}_{\beta}\vec{{\gamma}}\right)}\

(3.67)

where
\alpha=\{\xi^{\mu},b^{d},u^{\delta},t^{d}\}
,
\hat{\bf Z}_{1}
is defined as in (
3.13
) and we have defined

\displaystyle\vec{{\gamma}}^{\,t}=

\displaystyle\left(\begin{matrix}\rho_{0}&{\gamma}_{a}&{\tilde{\gamma}}^{a}&{\hat{\gamma}}_{\nu}&{\tilde{\rho}}\end{matrix}\right)\,,

(3.68)

\displaystyle\partial_{\xi^{\mu}}\vec{{\gamma}}^{\,t}=

\displaystyle\left(\begin{matrix}h_{\mu}&0&0&0&0\end{matrix}\right)\,,

\displaystyle\partial_{b^{c}}\vec{{\gamma}}^{\,t}=

\displaystyle\left(\begin{matrix}\rho_{c}&{\mathcal{K}}_{acd}{\tilde{\rho}}^{d}&\delta^{a}_{c}{\tilde{\rho}}&0&0\end{matrix}\right)\,,

\displaystyle\partial_{u^{\alpha}}\vec{{\gamma}}^{\,t}=

\displaystyle\left(\begin{matrix}0&0&0&-{\tilde{\rho}}A{\mathcal{K}}{\partial}_{\alpha}\partial_{\nu}K-{\tilde{\rho}}{\cal K}{\partial}_{\alpha}\hat{\epsilon}^{p}_{\nu}&0\end{matrix}\right)\,,

\displaystyle\partial_{t^{c}}\vec{{\gamma}}^{\,t}=

\displaystyle\left(\begin{matrix}0&-2{\tilde{\rho}}C{\mathcal{K}}_{ac}&-{\tilde{\rho}}B\delta^{a}_{c}&-3{\tilde{\rho}}A{\mathcal{K}}_{c}\partial_{\nu}K&0\end{matrix}\right)\,.

Notice that (
3.67
) is a product of two vectors with a positive definite metric. Therefore it corresponds to a positive definite Hessian, in agreement with the fact that
V_{1}
is a sum of squares.
The matrix of second derivatives of
V_{2}
yields, by direct computation,

\boxed{\partial_{\alpha}\partial_{\beta}V_{2}\rvert_{\rm vac}=2\vec{\eta_{\alpha}}^{t}\hat{\bf Z}_{1}{\partial}_{\beta}\vec{\gamma}=2{\partial}_{\alpha}\vec{{\gamma}}^{\,t}\hat{\bf Z}_{1}\vec{\eta_{\beta}}}\,

(3.69)

where we have defined

\displaystyle\vec{\eta}_{\xi^{\mu}}^{\,t}=

\displaystyle\left(\begin{matrix}0&0&0&0&0\end{matrix}\right)\,,

(3.70)

\displaystyle\vec{\eta}_{b^{d}}^{\,t}={\tilde{\rho}}

\displaystyle\left(\begin{matrix}0&0&\frac{3C}{{\mathcal{K}}}K^{bc}{\mathcal{K}}_{cd}&0&0\end{matrix}\right)\,,

\displaystyle\vec{\eta}_{u^{\alpha}}^{\,t}=

\displaystyle{\tilde{\rho}}\left(\begin{matrix}0&C{\partial}_{\alpha}K{\mathcal{K}}_{a}&B{\partial}_{\alpha}Kt^{a}&\left(\frac{2}{3}-4A\right)\frac{{\mathcal{K}}}{4}\left({\partial}_{\alpha}{\partial}_{\mu}K-{\partial}_{\mu}K{\partial}_{\alpha}K\right)+e^{-K}{\mathcal{K}}{\tilde{\rho}}K_{\beta\mu}{\partial}_{\alpha}\left(e^{K}K^{\gamma\beta}{\hat{\epsilon}}^{p}_{\gamma}\right)&0\end{matrix}\right)\,,

\displaystyle\vec{\eta}_{t^{d}}^{\,t}=

\displaystyle{\tilde{\rho}}\left(\begin{matrix}0&\frac{4C{\mathcal{K}}}{3}K_{bd}&\frac{3B}{2{\mathcal{K}}}K^{bc}{\mathcal{K}}_{cd}&{\mathcal{K}}{\partial}_{t^{d}}{\hat{\epsilon}}^{p}_{\mu}&0\end{matrix}\right)\,.

Unlike (
3.67
), the term (
3.69
) is in general not definite, and may yield tachyonic directions. Putting both results together we find that the matrix of second derivatives is given by

\displaystyle\boxed{{\partial}_{\alpha}{\partial}_{\beta}V\rvert_{\rm vac}=2\left({\partial}_{\alpha}\vec{{\gamma}}^{t}\right)\hat{\bf Z}_{1}\left({\partial}_{\beta}\vec{{\gamma}}+\vec{\eta_{\beta}}\right)}\,

(3.71)

which can also be written as:

\displaystyle{\partial}_{\alpha}{\partial}_{\beta}V\rvert_{\rm vac}=\left({\partial}_{\alpha}\vec{{\gamma}_{r}}^{t}+\vec{\eta_{\alpha}}^{t}\right)\hat{\bf Z}_{1}\left({\partial}_{\beta}\vec{\gamma_{r}}+\vec{\eta_{\beta}}\right)+\left({\partial}_{\alpha}\vec{{\gamma}_{r}}^{t}\right)\hat{\bf Z}_{1}\left({\partial}_{\beta}\vec{{\gamma}_{r}}\right)-\vec{\eta_{\alpha}}^{t}\hat{\bf Z}_{1}\vec{\eta_{\beta}}\,.

(3.72)

3.3.2
Flux-induced masses and perturbative stability

The explicit form of the Hessian for the different branches
A1-S1
and
A2-S1
is given in Appendix
C.3
, where the computation of its physical eigenvalues along tachyonic directions is also performed. The relevant results for classical stability are summarised in table
3.2
:

Branch

Tachyons

Physical eigenvalues

Massless modes

A2-S1

0

-

2N

A1-S1, SUSY

N

m^{2}_{tach}=\frac{8}{9}m_{BF}^{2}

N

A1-S1, Non-SUSY

N+1

m^{2}_{tach}=\frac{8}{9}m_{BF}^{2}

N

Table 3.2:
Massless and tachyonic modes for the extrema in the branch
S1
. Here
N
stands for the number of complex structure moduli. The extra zero modes in the branch
A2-S1
are discussed in appendix
B.3
.

Let us highlight some of the features resulting from this analysis:

-

Each vacuum has at least
N
zero modes, which are the complex structure axions that do not appear in the superpotential (
2.30
). As such, they do not appear in the F-term classical scalar potential, as one can check directly from eqs.(
4.2
)-(
3.3
). Therefore they constitute
N
flat directions of the classical potential. These unlifted axions may be eaten by D6-brane gauge bosons via the Stückelberg mechanism
[
33
]
.

-

As expected from the analysis in
[
115
]
, there are
N
tachyons with mass
\frac{8}{9}|m_{BF}|^{2}
in supersymmetric vacua. Such modes correspond to the saxionic directions that pair up with the flat axionic directions into complex fields. That is, they correspond to the saxions that do not appear in the superpotential (
2.30
).

-

As shown in appendix
B.2
the same tachyons are present in the non-supersymmetric vacua within the branch
A1-S1
, with the same mass in terms of the BF bound. Moreover, such non-SUSY vacua contain an extra tachyon which is a combination of complex and Kähler axionic directions, with exactly the same mass as the rest.

-

All these tachyons are absent in the
A2-S1
branch of solutions. Indeed, as shown in appendix
B.1
, all the solutions of this branch have a positive semidefinite Hessian. The tachyonic modes of the saxionic sector of the branch
A1-S1
are zero modes in this branch. They are however not flat directions and develop a positive quartic potential, see appendix
B.3
for a detailed discussion. The rest of the spectrum does not arrange into mass-degenerate complex scalars.

-

A general analysis is more involved for the
S2
branches. Following
[
33
]
, we have analysed them for the particular case of isotropic toroidal compactifications (i.e., where all three complex structure and three Kähler moduli are identified as
U_{i}=U
and
T_{i}=T
, respectively). We have found that AdS solutions in this branch contain tachyons not satisfying the BF bound, and are therefore perturbatively unstable. It would be interesting to see if this feature is also present for more general solutions and compactifications within this branch.

3.4
Validity of the solutions

In the following we analyse the validity of our solutions from the 4d perspective. We relegate the analysis from the 10d perspective to chapters
5
and
6
.

3.4.1
4d analysis and swampland conjectures

Since the different branches of solutions have been found via a classical potential
V
, one should check that they fall in the compactification regime in which the corrections to
V
are negligible. More precisely, a necessary condition to trust the above solutions is that the Kähler moduli are stabilised at sufficiently large values - so that
\alpha^{\prime}
corrections can be neglected - and the string coupling at small enough values - so that quantum corrections can also be neglected. In the following we will generalise the 4d validity analysis made in
[
50
,
33
]
to our solutions, obtaining similar results. In short, the scaling of the volumes and couplings with the fluxes follows the same pattern as in these references, which allows to fall in the required regime for large values of the shifted four-form flux. Indeed, we have that

\displaystyle\ell_{s}^{-1}\hat{e}_{a}={\tilde{\rho}}{\cal K}_{a}\left(C-\frac{1}{2}B^{2}\right)\quad\rightarrow\quad t^{2}\left(C-\frac{B^{2}}{2}\right)\sim\frac{\hat{e}}{m}\ ,

(3.73)

\displaystyle\hat{\rho}_{\mu}={\tilde{\rho}}{\mathcal{K}}A{\partial}_{\mu}K\quad\rightarrow\quad u\sim\frac{t^{3}mA}{h}\,,

(3.74)

where for simplicity we have assumed isotropic fluxes
h_{\mu}\sim h
,
\hat{e}_{a}\sim\hat{e}
. Since the
\hat{e}_{a}
are unconstrained by tadpole equations, in principle we are free to scale them to be as large as needed. Assuming that
2C\neq B^{2}
, the moduli dependence on this scaling is given by

\displaystyle t\sim\hat{e}^{1/2}\,,

\displaystyle u

\displaystyle\sim\hat{e}^{3/2}\,.

(3.75)

In addition we have that

\displaystyle e^{-4D}\sim u^{4}\sim\hat{e}^{6}\rightarrow e^{D}\sim\hat{e}^{-3/2}\,,

\displaystyle e^{\phi}

\displaystyle=\sqrt{{\rm Vol}_{\mathcal{M}_{6}}}e^{D}\sim t^{-3/2}\sim\hat{e}^{-3/4}\,.

(3.76)

These are the same scaling relations found in
[
50
]
and so, for large
\hat{e}
, we are in a regime of large volume and weak coupling that prevents large corrections. Finally, one can check that the four-form density scaling is similar to
[
50
]
and therefore the corresponding higher derivative corrections are equally suppressed.

Additionally, one can check the scaling of the different mass scales, following for instance the relations given in
[
65
]
:

\displaystyle\frac{M_{\rm KK}}{M_{\rm P}}

\displaystyle\sim\frac{g_{s}}{V^{2/3}}\sim t^{-7/2}\sim\hat{e}^{-7/4}\,,

\displaystyle\frac{\Lambda}{M_{\rm P}^{2}}

\displaystyle\sim e^{K}{\mathcal{K}}^{2}m^{2}\sim\frac{t^{3}}{u^{4}}\sim t^{-3}\sim\hat{e}^{-9/2}\,,

(3.77)

\displaystyle R_{\rm AdS}M_{\rm P}

\displaystyle\sim\Lambda^{-1/2}M_{\rm P}\sim\hat{e}^{9/4}\,.

We then recover the same scaling as found in
[
50
]
, and in particular the same parametric separation between the compactification scale and the AdS radius:

\displaystyle\frac{R_{\rm AdS}}{R_{\rm KK}}

\displaystyle\sim\hat{e}^{1/2}\,,

\displaystyle\hat{R}_{\rm KK}\sim\hat{R}_{\rm AdS}^{7/9}\ \longrightarrow\ \frac{{M}_{\rm KK}}{M_{\rm P}}\sim\hat{\Lambda}^{7/18}\,,

(3.78)

where
\hat{R}=RM_{\rm P}
and
\hat{\Lambda}=\Lambda/M_{\rm P}^{2}
. Lastly, using the results derived in appendix
B.2
, for the
A1-S1
branch we have:

m^{2}_{\text{moduli}}\sim m_{BF}^{2}\sim\Lambda\sim\frac{1}{R_{AdS}^{2}}\,,

(3.79)

where
m^{2}_{\text{moduli}}
refers to the canonically normalised mass of the moduli becoming massive.

Regarding the swampland conjectures, the last relation (
3.79
) satisfies the criterium suggested in
[
87
]
for the lightest scalars -recall section
2.3.2
-. It would be interesting to check if the spectrum of Stückelberg masses associated with the zero modes as well as the spectrum of the other branches still satisfy this relation. In terms of the AdS conjectures formulated in
[
77
]
, all the AdS vacua found in our analysis satisfy the plain AdS Distance Conjecture, while the supersymmetric ones would fail to satisfy its strong version. It was suggested in
[
77
]
that this failure could be related to the lack of knowledge of the full 10d supergravity background describing such vacua -which are only satisfied in the smearing approximation- and that the back-reaction of the sources could spoil the separation of scales. In fact, to date the absence of a solution to the 10d equations of motion holds for each of the AdS vacua found in our 4d analysis, and is to be expected that finding their 10d description is at the same level of difficulty. This problem will be addressed in detail in chapters
5
-
6
. Finally, there is also the non-Supersymmetric AdS Instability Conjecture, which predicts that the
\mathcal{N}=0
vacua found should suffer from instabilities. We have seen in the previous section that these non-susy AdS vacua are stable at the classical level. We will examine branch
A1-S1
in light of this conjecture in chapter
7

3.5
Including mobile D6-branes

As in
[
116
,
117
,
64
]
we may generalise the above setup by considering type IIA orientifold compactifications where D6-branes have deformation and Wilson line moduli. In order to preserve supersymmetry such D6-branes must wrap special Lagrangian three-cycles
\Pi_{\alpha}\subset{\cal M}_{6}
with vanishing worldvolume flux
[
118
,
119
]
. Then the open string moduli space is characterised by
b_{1}(\Pi_{\alpha})
complex moduli
[
120
,
121
]
. These are defined as
[
31
,
65
]

\Phi_{\alpha}^{i}=T^{a}f_{\alpha\,a}^{i}-\theta^{i}_{\alpha}=\hat{\theta}_{\alpha}^{i}+i\,\phi^{i}_{\alpha}\,,

(3.80)

where
i
runs over the integer harmonic one-forms
\zeta_{i}
of
\Pi_{\alpha}
,
\theta^{i}_{\alpha}
is the Wilson line corresponding to each of them and
f_{\alpha\,a}^{i}
is a function of the corresponding geometric deformation of
\Pi_{\alpha}
defined in terms of a chain integral. We refer the reader to
[
64
,
31
,
65
]
for further details on these definitions.

For each harmonic one-form
\zeta_{i}\in H^{1}(\Pi_{\alpha},\mathbb{Z})
there is a two-form
\eta^{i}\in H^{2}(\Pi_{\alpha},\mathbb{Z})
along with a worldvolume flux
F=n_{F\,i}^{\alpha}
that can be turned on. This enters the D6-brane DBI action and therefore the scalar potential in the combination
n_{F\,i}^{\alpha}-\frac{1}{2}g_{i\,{\alpha}}^{\mu}h_{\mu}
, where
g_{i\,{\alpha}}^{\mu}
is also defined in terms of a chain integral
[
64
,
31
,
65
]
. As such the presence of such fluxes generates a potential, captured by the superpotential

\ell_{s}{W}_{\rm D6}\,=\,\Phi_{\alpha}^{i}(n_{F\,i}^{\alpha}-n_{a\,i}^{\alpha}T^{a})+\ell_{s}W_{0}\,,

(3.81)

where

n_{a\,i}^{\alpha}=\frac{1}{\ell_{s}^{3}}\int_{\Pi_{\alpha}}\omega_{a}\wedge\zeta_{i}\in\mathbb{Z}

(3.82)

are non-vanishing whenever the two-cycles of
\Pi_{\alpha}
are non-trivial in
H_{2}(\mathcal{M}_{6},\mathbb{Z})
. Indeed, as pointed out in
[
122
]
in this case the open string moduli develop a potential due to the D6-brane backreaction on a compact space.

An important effect to take into account is the field redefinition of the closed string moduli in the dilaton-complex structure sector in the presence of open string moduli. We have that the new variables read
[
31
,
65
]

U^{\mu}=U_{\star}^{\mu}+\frac{1}{2}\sum_{\alpha}\left(g^{\mu}_{i\,{\alpha}}{\theta}^{i}_{\alpha}-T^{a}H_{a\,{\alpha}}^{\mu}\right)\,,

(3.83)

where
U_{\star}^{\mu}
stand for the complex structure moduli in the absence of mobile D6-branes, namely (
2.19
), and
U^{\mu}
are the redefined 4d variables. Finally
H_{a\,{\alpha}}^{\mu}
are functions of the saxions defined in terms of
f_{\alpha\,a}^{i}
and
g_{i\,{\alpha}}^{\mu}

[
31
]
. Notice that (
2.20
) is a function of
u^{\mu}_{\star}
